Low-dimensional adaptation of diffusion models: Convergence in total variation
Jiadong Liang, Zhihan Huang, Yuxin Chen

Abstract
This paper investigates how diffusion generative models leverage (unknown) low-dimensional structure to accelerate sampling. Focusing on two mainstream samplers — the denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM) and the denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM), we prove that their iteration complexities under exact score functions are at most the order of k / ε k/\varepsilon (up to log factor), where ε \varepsilon is the precision in total variation distance and k k is some intrinsic dimension of the target distribution. We further extend these convergence guarantees to the setting in which the score functions are learned from data rather than known exactly, showing that the convergence performance degrades gracefully under suitable score estimation assumptions. We then show that these assumptions are attainable via kernel-based score estimators with finite-sample guarantees that also adapt to the low-dimensional structure. Our results apply to a broad family of target distributions without requiring smoothness or log-concavity. Our findings provide the first rigorous evidence for the adaptivity of the DDIM-type samplers to unknown low-dimensional structure, and improve over the state-of-the-art DDPM theory regarding total variation convergence.



†
†
footnotetext:
Accepted for presentation at the Conference on Learning Theory (COLT) 2025.

1
Introduction

As a cornerstone of the rapidly evolving field of generative AI, diffusion generative models have driven mind-blowing progress across a diverse range of applications, such as image and video generation, medical image analysis, and time-series forecasting, to name just a few
(
Ramesh et al., 2022
;
Croitoru et al., 2023
;
Kazerouni et al., 2023
)
.
The remarkable effectiveness of diffusion models has inspired a recent wave of activity aimed at developing and strengthening their theoretical underpinnings.

1.1
Score-based generative modeling: DDPM and DDIM

At their core, diffusion models seek to gradually transform pure noise into new samples that emulate a
d
-dimensional target distribution
p_{\mathsf{data}}
, accomplished by learning to reverse a forward stochastic process that progressively converts data into noise, detailed below.

Forward process.

A common choice of the forward process with horizon
T
is given by

\displaystyle X_{0}\sim p_{\mathsf{data}},\qquad X_{t}=\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}\,X_{t-1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha_{t}}\,W_{t},\quad t=1,\cdots,T,

(1)

where
\{W_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
are independent noise vectors obeying
W_{i}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,I_{d})
,
and the sequence
\{\alpha_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}\subseteq(0,1)

controls the variance of the Gaussian noise injected in each step.
Informally, as
T
grows,
the distribution of
X_{t}
typically converges to the standard Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.

Reverse process and diffusion-based samplers.

As it turns out,
the forward Markov process (
1
) is reversible in general, a property that follows from classical results in the stochastic differential equation (SDE) literature
(
Anderson, 1982
;
Haussmann and Pardoux, 1986
)
.
This intriguing property underpins the data generation process of diffusion models,
which involves constructing a reverse process
Y_{T}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{0}

that closely mimics the forward process (
1
) in the sense that
Y_{t}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\approx}X_{t}
for each step
t
.
Crucially, the reversal of the forward process hinges upon access to the so-called (Stein) score function

\displaystyle s_{t}^{\star}(X)\coloneqq\nabla\log p_{X_{t}}(X)

(2)

— hence the term “score-based generative modeling.”
To formalize the sampling process,
one needs to specify the initialization and iterative steps of the reverse process.
The initialization step is straightforward:
given that
X_{T}
is approximately Gaussian for large enough
T
, one generic choice is to draw
Y_{T}
as pure noise
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.
As such, a key step underlying the design of the sampling process boils down to how to update
Y_{t}
at each step while maintaining the desired distributional proximity.
In what follows, we single out two mainstream paradigms, assuming availability of an estimate
s_{t}
of the true score function
s_{t}^{\star}
at each
t
:

•

Denoising Diffusion Implicit Model (DDIM).

The DDIM sampler (or the probability flow ODE sampler)
(
Song et al., 2020
)
adopts a deterministic update rule below:

\displaystyle Y_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),\qquad Y_{t-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}}\big(Y_{t}+\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}s_{t}(Y_{t})\big),\qquad t=T,\cdots,1,

(3)

where
\{\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}\}
represents some suitably chosen coefficients.
In words, each step (
3
) computes
Y_{t-1}
as a weighted sum of
Y_{t}
and its score estimate.

•

Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM).
Originally proposed by
Ho et al. (2020)
as a way to optimize certain variational lower bounds on the log-likelihood, DDPM employs the following stochastic iterative updates:

\displaystyle Y_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),\qquad Y_{t-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}}\big(Y_{t}+\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}s_{t}(Y_{t})+\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}Z_{t}\big),\quad t=T,\cdots,1,

(4)

where the
Z_{t}
’s are independently generated obeying
Z_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, and
\{\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}\}
and
\{\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}\}
are properly chosen coefficients.
A key distinction from DDIM is that the iterative updates (
4
) inject additional stochastic noise at each step.

1.2
Harnessing low-dimensional structure?

Motivated by the practical efficacy of diffusion models, the past few years have witnessed a flurry of activity towards establishing convergence theory for both DDPM and DDIM
(
Lee et al., 2022
;
Chen et al., 2022b
;
Chen et al., 2023a
;
Chen et al., 2023d
;
Chen et al., 2024b
;
Benton et al., 2024
;
Benton et al., 2023
;
Li et al., 2024b
;
Li et al., 2024c
;
Li et al., 2024d
;
Gao and Zhu, 2024
;
Huang et al., 2024
;
Li and Yan, 2024b
;
Liang et al., 2024
;
Li and Jiao, 2024
)
. For a fairly general family of target distributions
p_{\mathsf{data}}
(without assuming smoothness and log-concavity), the state-of-the-art theory
Li and Yan (2024a)
;
Li et al. (2024c)
demonstrated that for both DDPM and DDIM, it takes at most the order of (modulo some log factor)

\displaystyle d/\varepsilon~\text{ iterations }

(5)

to yield a sample whose distribution is
\varepsilon
-close in total variation (TV) distance to the target distribution,
provided that perfect score function estimates are available.

Nevertheless, even linear scaling in
d
can be prohibitively expensive for many contemporary applications.
Take the ImageNet dataset
(
Deng et al., 2009
)
for instance: each image might contain 150,528 pixels,
while its intrinsic dimension is estimated to be 43 or less
(
Pope et al., 2021
)
.
As a result, applying the state-of-the-art theory (
5
) could suggest an iteration complexity that exceeds one million,
even though practical implementations of DDIM and DDPM often produce high-quality samples in just a few hundred (or even a few ten) iterations.
The discrepancy between theory and practice suggests that worst-case bounds, such as (
5
), may be overly conservative.
To reconcile this discrepancy, it is crucial to bear in mind the intrinsic dimension of the target data distribution and explore whether and how diffusion models can harness this potentially low-dimensional structure.

The development of diffusion model theory that can effectively account for low dimensionality is, however, still in its early stages. For example, the ability of DDIM to adapt to low-dimensional structure was previously completely out of reach in theory, despite its widespread use.
The situation for DDPM is more advanced: recent work

Li and Yan (2024a)
;
Azangulov et al. (2024)
;
Potaptchik et al. (2024)
;
Huang et al. (2026)
explored its low-dimensional adaptation capability, assuming that the target data distribution is supported on some low-dimensional structure like a manifold. These studies focused primarily on convergence in Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence,
which
is known to yield loose results when directly translated into convergence guarantees based on other metrics like the TV distance.

1.3
This paper

An overview of our contributions.

In this paper, we develop a new suite of total-variation-based theoretical guarantees for the DDIM and DDPM samplers to uncover how they leverage low-dimensional structure to accelerate sampling. Concretely,
consider a general definition of intrinsic dimension for the target distribution
p_{\mathsf{data}}
, such that the intrinsic dimension is
k
if the logarithm of the covering number of the support of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
is on the order of
k
(up to some log factor). Under this notion, we first prove in Theorem
1
that, with exact score functions, both DDPM and DDIM require at most the order of

\displaystyle k/\varepsilon~\text{ iterations ~~(up to log factor)}

(6)

to generate a sample that is
\varepsilon
-close in TV distance to the target distribution. This result applies to a broad class of target distributions without requiring restrictive assumptions such as smoothness or log-concavity. For those applications where
k\ll d
— a situation that is prevalent in many modern-day applications — our theory underscores the striking capability of diffusion models to automatically exploit the favorable intrinsic structure of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
without explicitly modeling the low-dimensional structure or altering the algorithms.

Next, we extend these guarantees to the more realistic setting in which the score functions are learned from data rather than known exactly. Theorems
2
,
4
and
5
show that, under suitable score estimation assumptions,
the convergence performance of both DDIM and DDPM degrade gracefully as the score estimation accuracy worsens. We further establish that these score estimation assumptions are achievable by kernel-based score estimators, deriving finite-sample guarantees that automatically adapt to the underlying low-dimensional structure of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
(see Theorems
3
and
6
).

Furthermore, we illuminate the specific coefficient choices of the DDIM/DDPM samplers, by linking them with reverse-time differential equations with specific discretization to exploit low dimensionality.
We also develop a lower bound for a single step of the discretized reverse process, which unravels the necessity and optimality of the coefficient designs proposed originally by
Ho et al. (2020)
;
Song et al. (2020)
.

It is worth emphasizing that
our results provide the first theory justifying the low-dimensional adaptation ability of the DDIM-type samplers, and significantly improve over the state-of-the-art DDPM theory regarding total variation convergence; see Table
1
and Table
2
for detailed comparisons with prior DDIM and DDPM theory, respectively.
†
†

Note that in a large fraction of prior DDPM theory, the bound based on the TV distance is obtained by applying Pinsker’s inequality (i.e.,
\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{1}},q_{Y_{1}})\leq\sqrt{2\mathsf{KL}(p_{X_{1}}\parallel p_{Y_{1}})}
).

Notation.

For any positive integer
n
, let
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r>0
, define
\mathcal{B}(x,r)\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|z-x\|_{2}\leq r\}
as the closed
\ell_{2}
-neighborhood of radius
r
centered at
x
.
For two functions
f
and
g
, we employ the notation
f=O(g)
or
f\lesssim g
to mean that there exists some universal constant
C>0
such that
f\leq Cg
.
The notation
f=\widetilde{O}(g)
is defined analogously except that the log dependency is hidden. Additionally,
f\gtrsim g
means
g\lesssim f
, and
f\asymp g
means
f\lesssim g
and
g\lesssim f
hold at once.
For any two distributions
p
and
q
, we denote by
\mathsf{TV}(p,q)
(resp.
\mathsf{KL}(p\parallel q)
) the TV distance (resp. KL divergence) between
p
and
q
.
We denote by
p_{X_{t}}
and
p_{Y_{t}}
the probability density function of
X_{t}
and
Y_{t}
, respectively.
For any matrix
A
, we denote by
\|A\|
(resp.
\|A\|_{\mathrm{F}}
) the spectral norm (resp. Frobenius norm) of
A
, and
\mathrm{tr}(A)
the trace of
A
. For any vector
A
, write
A^{\otimes 2}\coloneqq AA^{\top}
.
For any vector-valued function
f(x)
, we let
\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}
represent the Jacobian matrix of
f(x)
; for any real-valued function
g(x)
, we let
\nabla g(x)
represent the gradient of
g(x)
. Also, for any random object
X
, we denote by
\mathsf{supp}(X)
the support of
X
.
For any two
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, define
a\wedge b\coloneqq\min\{a,b\}
.

2
Preliminaries

Before proceeding to our formal theory and analysis, we briefly overview some basics and the operational mechanism of diffusion models, covering both DDIM and DDPM.

Forward process and noise schedule.

As previously described in (
1
),
the forward process progressively injects Gaussian noise to transform the target distribution
p_{\mathsf{data}}
into a pure noise distribution that is easy to sample from. The Gaussian nature of the injected noise allows for a more direct relation between
X_{0}
and
X_{t}
as follows:

X_{t}=\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}X_{0}+\sqrt{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\,\overline{W}_{t}\qquad\text{ with }\overline{W}_{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,I_{d}),

(7)

where we introduce the following parameters for any
1\leq t\leq T
:

\displaystyle\overline{\alpha}_{t}\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{t}\alpha_{i}.

(8)

As it turns out, the choices of the coefficients
\{\alpha_{t}\}
play an important role in determining the convergence properties of diffusion models.
Here and throughout, we adopt the choices used in the previous work
(
Li et al., 2024c
;
Li and Yan, 2024a
;
Li and Yan, 2024b
)
:

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1-\alpha_{1}=\frac{1}{T^{c_{0}}},

(9a)

\displaystyle\beta_{t+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1-\alpha_{t+1}=\frac{c_{1}\log T}{T}\min\left\{\beta_{1}\left(1+\frac{c_{1}\log T}{T}\right)^{t},1\right\},\quad 1\leq t<T,

(9b)

where
c_{0},c_{1}>0
are some large enough numerical constants.
In words, this noise variance schedule (as
\beta_{t}
is the variance of the noise injected at step
t
) contains two phases: it grows exponentially at the beginning, and then stays flat after reaching the order of
\frac{\log T}{T}
,
which is consistent with the state-of-the-art diffusion model theory (e.g.,
Benton et al. (2024)
;
Potaptchik et al. (2024)
;
Huang et al. (2026)
;
Li et al. (2024c)
).

Score-based generative models.

Next, we describe the precise update rules for both DDIM-type and DDPM-type samplers.

\bullet

DDIM-type samplers.

As mentioned previously, a DDIM-type sampler starts with
Y_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and adopts the following deterministic update rule:

\displaystyle Y_{t-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}}\big(Y_{t}+\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}s_{t}(Y_{t})\big),\qquad t=T,\cdots,1.

(10)

Here,
\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}
is a design parameter that admits multiple alternatives, and
we list a couple of choices used in previous literature:

\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{1+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}}

(original DDIM
(
Song et al., 2020
)
;
this work
)

(11a)

\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{2}

(
Li et al., 2024c
;
Li et al., 2024d
)

(11b)

\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}=

\displaystyle\frac{-1+4\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}-3\alpha_{t}}{2\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}}

(
Song et al., 2021
)

(11c)

Importantly, all of these parameter choices lead to samplers that are asymptotically consistent — meaning that the distribution of the sampling output converges to the target data distribution as
T
grows — under mild conditions on the target data distribution.
In this paper, we concentrate on the parameter schedule (
11a
) proposed in the original DDIM paper
(
Song et al., 2020
)
.

\bullet

DDPM-type samplers.

A DDPM-type sampler adopts the initialization
Y_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and implements the stochastic update rule:

\displaystyle Y_{t-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}}\big(Y_{t}+\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}s_{t}(Y_{t})+\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}Z_{t}\big),\qquad t=T,\cdots,1,

(12)

with independent noise
Z_{t}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.
Here,
\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}
and
\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}
are design parameters, with several choices listed below:

\displaystyle(\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}},\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}})=

\displaystyle\left(1-\alpha_{t},\sqrt{\frac{(1-\alpha_{t})(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}\right)

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\text{original DDPM \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{ho2020denoising}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})};}\\
\text{\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{potaptchik2024linear}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}};}\\
\text{\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{huang2024denoising}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}};}\\
\text{a special case of \lx@text@underline{this work}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle(\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}},\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}})=

\displaystyle\big(\,2(1-\sqrt{\alpha}_{t}),\sqrt{1-\alpha_{t}}\,\big)

(
Benton et al., 2024
;
Chen et al., 2023a
)

(13f)

\displaystyle(\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}},\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}})=

\displaystyle\left(1-\alpha_{t},\sqrt{1-\alpha_{t}}\right)

(
Li et al., 2023a
;
Li and Yan, 2024b
)

(13g)

All of the above choices come with convergence theory guaranteeing asymptotic consistency. In this work, we would like to accommodate a range of parameter schedules that subsumes as a special case the one (
13
) proposed in the original DDPM paper
(
Ho et al., 2020
)
.

ODE and SDE perspectives.

To shed light on the rationale and feasibility of the DDIM-type and DDPM-type samplers, it is helpful to look at the continuous-time analogs of both forward and backward processes and resort to the toolbox of ordinary differential equations (ODEs) and stochastic differential equations (SDEs). We briefly review some basics in the sequel, and will illuminate deeper connections in Section
A
.

\bullet

Forward SDE.

The forward process (
7
) is intimately connected with the following continuous-time process with some specific choice of
\beta(t)
:

{\textnormal{d}}X_{t}=-\beta(t)X_{t}{\textnormal{d}}t+\sqrt{2\beta(t)}\,{\textnormal{d}}B_{t},

(14)

where
(B_{t})
represents a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In fact, standard SDE theory reveals that SDE (
14
) admits the following characterization

X_{t}=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}\beta(s){\textnormal{d}}s\right)X_{0}+\sqrt{1-\exp\bigg(-2\int_{0}^{t}\beta(s){\textnormal{d}}s\bigg)}\overline{W}_{t}

(15)

for some
\overline{W}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
,
whose marginal distribution coincides with that of (
7
) if

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}=\exp\left(-2\int_{t-1}^{t}\beta(s){\textnormal{d}}s\right),\qquad t=1,\cdots,T.

(16)

\bullet

Probability flow ODE or diffusion ODE.
One way to reverse the forward process is through the probability flow ODE
(
Song et al., 2021
)
(also known as diffusion ODE):

{\textnormal{d}}Y_{t}=\left(Y_{t}+s^{\star}_{T-t}(Y_{t})\right)\beta(T-t){\textnormal{d}}t,\qquad t\in[0,T],

(17)

which enjoys matching marginal distribution
Y_{T-t}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}X_{t}
for all
0\leq t\leq T
as long as we generate
Y_{0}\sim p_{X_{T}}
.
To approximately simulate this reverse ODE in practice and obtain a tractable sampler, a common strategy is to perform time discretization of ODE (
17
).
Note that different discretization schemes can result in different design coefficients
\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}
as in the DDIM-type update rule (
10
).

\bullet

Reverse-time SDE.

An alternative way to reverse the forward process is via a properly chosen SDE.
In view of the classical results in the SDE literature
(
Anderson, 1982
;
Haussmann and Pardoux, 1986
)
, the following SDE,

{\textnormal{d}}Y_{t}=\left(Y_{t}+2s^{\star}_{T-t}(Y_{t})\right)\beta(T-t){\textnormal{d}}t+\sqrt{2\beta(T-t)}\,{\textnormal{d}}W_{t},\qquad t\in[0,T]

(18)

with
(W_{t})
a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
reverses the forward process (
14
) in the sense that
Y_{T-t}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}X_{t}
for all
0\leq t\leq T
as long as
Y_{0}\sim p_{X_{T}}
.
Akin to the DDIM counterpart, the DDPM-type samplers can often be viewed as time discretization of SDE (
18
),
and different discretization schemes correspond to different coefficient choices of
(\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}},\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}})
as in (
12
).

\bullet

Generalized reverse-time ODE/SDE.

We also consider the more general form:

{\textnormal{d}}Y_{t}=\left(Y_{t}+\big(1+\xi(T-t)\big)s^{\star}_{T-t}(Y_{t})\right)\beta(T-t){\textnormal{d}}t+\sqrt{2\xi(T-t)\beta(T-t)}\,{\textnormal{d}}W_{t},

(19)

t\in[0,T]
,
for some general function
\xi(t)\geq 0
for all
0\leq t\leq T
, where
(W_{t})
again represents a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We shall formally demonstrate the desired distributional property of this family of differential equations in Appendix
F.1
.
When
\xi(t)=0
(resp.
\xi(t)=1
) for all
t\in[0,T]
, (
19
) reduces to ODE (
17
) (resp. SDE (
18
)).
For a general
\xi(t)
, suitable time discretizationschemes of (
19
) can lead to new samplers other than the original DDIM and DDPM.

3
Main results

This section presents our main theory.
We begin in Section
3.1
by establishing performance guarantees for both DDIM and DDPM under the idealized setting where exact scores are available. We then turn to the more practical setting in which only approximate score estimates are accessible. Sections
3.2.1
and
3.2.2
analyze DDIM and DDPM, respectively, establishing convergence guarantees for data generation under approximate scores, accompanied by adaptive, finite-sample guarantees for score estimation. Section
3.3

develops a lower bound that demonstrates the optimality of the coefficients in DDIM and DDPM.

3.1
Theoretical guarantees under exact scores

We first study the idealized setting in which the ground-truth score functions are available, i.e.,
s_{t}=s_{t}^{\star}
, and establish convergence guarantees for both DDIM and DDPM samplers.

3.1.1
Assumptions

Our theory relies on two assumptions about the target distribution
p_{\mathsf{data}}
.
The first assumption requires the support of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
— denoted by

\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{data}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
— to be (polynomially) bounded.

Assumption 1
(Polynomially bounded support)

Suppose that there exists a universal constant
c_{R}>0
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{data}}}\left\|{x}\right\|_{2}\leq R_{0}~~~\text{ for some }1\leq R_{0}\leq T^{c_{R}}.

Remark 1

Note that the size of the support
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{data}}
is allowed to scale polynomially (with an arbitrarily large constant degree) in the number of iterations of the sampler, which accommodates a very wide range of practical applications like image generation.

Next, we formalize the notion of
low-dimensional structure
of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
.
To rigorously define its “intrinsic dimension,” we introduce the following definition of covering number
(
Wainwright, 2019
, Chapter 5)
, a standard and widely used measure of the complexity of a set
\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 1
(Covering number)

For any set
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
the (Euclidean) covering number at scale
\epsilon_{0}>0
, denoted by
N_{\epsilon_{0}}(\mathcal{X})
, is defined as the smallest integer
n
such that there exist points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
obeying

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}(x_{i},\epsilon_{0}).

The covering number in turn enables a flexible characterization of the complexity of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
.

Assumption 2
(Intrinsic dimension)

For any
\epsilon_{0}>0
, the covering number of the support
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{data}}
of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
is assumed to satisfy

\log N_{\epsilon_{0}}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{data}})\leq C_{\mathsf{cover}}k\log\left(\frac{R_{0}}{\epsilon_{0}}\right)

for some universal constant
C_{\mathsf{cover}}>0
, where
R_{0}
is the support radius in Assumption
1
.
Here and throughout, we shall refer to
k
as the intrinsic dimension of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
.

The intrinsic dimension defined above is fairly generic, facilitating studies of a number of important low-dimensional structures.
Partial examples that satisfy Assumption
2
include
k
-dimensional linear subspace in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

k
-dimensional manifolds
(provided that
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{data}}
is polynomially bounded as in Assumption
1
), as well as structures with doubling dimension
k

(
Dasgupta and Freund, 2008
)
.
See
Huang et al. (2026, Section 4.1)
for more detailed discussion.

3.1.2
Convergence theory under exact scores

Armed with the above assumptions — which encompass a broad class of target distributions — we now establish the performance guarantees of DDIM and DDPM under exact scores.

Theorem 1

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
2
hold, and that the score estimates are exact (i.e.,
s_{t}=s_{t}^{\star}
for all
t=1,\ldots,T
). Then
the following results hold.

•

DDIM.

The DDIM sampler (
3
) with

\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}=\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{1+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}}

satisfies

\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{1}},p_{Y_{1}})\lesssim\frac{k\log^{3}T}{T}.

•

DDPM.

The DDPM sampler (
4
) with

\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}=1-\alpha_{t},\,\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}=\sqrt{\frac{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\alpha_{t})}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}

satisfies

\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{1}},p_{Y_{1}})\lesssim\frac{k\log^{3}T}{T}.

Encouragingly, Theorem
1
offers several key insights into the remarkable efficacy of DDIM and DDPM when the target distribution exhibits
unknown
low dimensionality.

•

Adaptation to intrinsic dimension.

Theorem
1
shows that both DDIM and DDPM can
adapt to the low-dimensional structure of the data distribution when the true scores
are accessible. In particular, both samplers achieve an iteration complexity of

\widetilde{O}(k/\varepsilon)
for reaching

\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{1}},p_{Y_{1}})\leq\varepsilon
, exhibiting a
linear
dependence on the intrinsic dimension
k
. For DDIM, to the best of our
knowledge, this is the first result showing adaptation to unknown
low-dimensional structure. For DDPM, such low-dimensional adaptation was first
studied by
Li and Yan (2024a)
; in comparison, our TV-based theory establishes
a linear scaling on the intrinsic dimension
k
that significantly improves upon
Li and Yan (2024a)
. See
Tables
1
and
2
for comparisons with prior results.

•

Minimal distributional assumptions.
The low-dimensional adaptation capability established by our theory holds under remarkably broad distributional assumptions, requiring neither smoothness nor log-concavity. This broad applicability is aligned with the
practical success of diffusion models, which are routinely used to model and generate samples from rich and
highly complex data distributions.

•

No burn-in cost.

We also compare Theorem
2
with the state-of-the-art DDIM theory
(
Li et al., 2024c
)
for the case with
k=d
and exact scores. Recall that
Li et al. (2024c)
established an
\widetilde{O}(d/T)
convergence rate,
which is consistent with Theorem
2
. Nevertheless, the theory therein requires a burn-in cost
T\gtrsim d^{2}\log^{5}T
; in comparison, our theorem does not impose such a burn-in requirement.

•

Coefficient choices.

As it turns out, the remarkable low-dimensional adaptation capability of diffusion models is achieved using the
classical coefficient choices. For DDIM, the coefficient

\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}=\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{1+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}}

matches exactly that proposed in the original DDIM sampler

(
Song et al., 2020
)
. Likewise, for DDPM, the coefficients

\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}=1-\alpha_{t},\,\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}=\sqrt{\frac{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\alpha_{t})}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}

coincide with those introduced in the original DDPM paper
Ho et al. (2020)
. Our results therefore provide a theoretical explanation for the practical success of these classical coefficient choices: they are naturally aligned with the low-dimensional structure of the target distribution, enabling both samplers to adapt automatically to such favorable structure.

3.2
Theoretical guarantees under noisy score estimates

We now turn to the more realistic setting in which the scores are estimated from data (and hence noisy) rather than known exactly. To characterize the impact of score estimation accuracy on the performance of diffusion samplers, we introduce additional assumptions that quantify the quality of the estimated scores, and establish convergence guarantees for both DDIM and DDPM accordingly. We further show how such assumptions can be attained by statistical estimation procedures and derive finite-sample guarantees that also adapt to the underlying low-dimensional structure.

3.2.1
DDIM theory under noisy scores

Score estimation assumptions.

We first analyze the TV-based performance of the DDIM sampler in the presence of noisy score estimates. Our theory relies on the following assumptions on the estimated scores. Compared with those required for DDPM (see Section
3.2.2
), these assumptions are more stringent due to the deterministic nature of DDIM, but, as we will shortly justify, they are attainable by kernel-based score estimators with finite-sample and adaptive guarantees.

Assumption 3
(Score estimates for DDIM)

Suppose that each of the estimated score functions
\{s_{t}(\cdot)\}_{t=1}^{T}
is twice continuously differentiable. For each
1\leq t\leq T
, assume that

\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}v^{\top}\nabla s_{t}(x)v\geq-\frac{1}{4}\left\|{v}\right\|_{2}^{2}\qquad\text{for all }v,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

(20a)

Further, we suppose that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{score},t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddim}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{ with }\varepsilon_{\mathsf{score},t}^{2}\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}\big[\left\|{s_{t}(X_{t})-s_{t}^{\star}(X_{t})}\right\|_{2}^{2}\big],

(20b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},1,t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},1}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{ with }\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},1,t}^{2}\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\left\|{\frac{\partial s_{t}(X_{t})}{\partial x}-\frac{\partial s_{t}^{\star}(X_{t})}{\partial x}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\bigg],

(20c)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},2,t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},2}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{ with }\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},2,t}^{2}\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\mathrm{tr}\bigg(\frac{\partial s_{t}(X_{t})}{\partial x}-\frac{\partial s_{t}^{\star}(X_{t})}{\partial x}\bigg)^{2}\bigg],

(20d)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Hess},t}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Hess}}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{ with }\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Hess},t}^{2}\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\left\|{\nabla\mathrm{tr}\bigg(\frac{\partial s_{t}(X_{t})}{\partial x}-\frac{\partial s_{t}^{\star}(X_{t})}{\partial x}\bigg)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigg].

(20e)

In essence, Assumption
3
is concerned with the accuracy of the estimated score functions under a few metrics,
including time-averaged errors for the score estimates themselves as well as their associated Jacobians and Hessians.
Intuitively, given
that DDIM is deterministic without injecting extra random noise to smooth the
trajectory, additional assumptions like higher-order score estimation accuracy
are needed in order to mitigate the propagation of estimation errors in each
backward step; see, e.g.,
Li et al. (2024c)
;
Huang et al. (2024)
. Moreover, we will show that the above assumptions are attainable via kernel-based score estimation procedures with adaptive finite-sample guarantees.

Convergence guarantees for DDIM.

We now present performance guarantees for the DDIM sampler when exact scores are inaccessible. The proof is postponed to Appendix
C
.

Theorem 2

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
, the DDIM sampler (
3
) with the coefficients
\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}=\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{1+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}}
yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{1}},p_{Y_{1}})\lesssim\frac{k\log^{3}T}{T}+(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddim}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},1}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},2}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Hess}})\sqrt{\log T}.

(21)

Compared with the exact-score setting, the TV distance of interest continues to converge at a rate
\widetilde{O}(k/T)
until it reaches an error floor determined by the error metrics in Assumption
3
.
Unlike previous analysis for DDIM, Theorem
2
makes an assumption about the second-order approximation of
s_{t}(\cdot)
to
s_{t}^{\star}(\cdot)
, i.e., the additional error term
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Hess}}
in Assumption
3
. This arises because, in prior studies, the score error terms in the convergence rate of DDIM were dependent on the ambient dimension
d
. For instance, in
Huang et al. (2024)
;
Li et al. (2024c)
, the estimation error terms in their respective convergence rates are given by
d^{\frac{3}{4}}L^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{score}}
and
\sqrt{d}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{score}}+d\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi}}
, where
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{score}}
and
L
denote certain
\ell_{2}
score error and smoothness parameter, respectively. In comparison, in our Theorem
2
, the score error term in the sampling error is nearly dimension-free (except for log dependency), meaning that this error almost does not amplify when the intrinsic and ambient dimensions increase.

Adaptive score estimation guarantees.

While Assumption
3
enables the convergence theory in
Theorem
2
, a natural question is whether it can be satisfied with finite samples, and if so, by what statistical procedures. We now address this question by showing that Assumption
3
is attainable with finite-sample guarantees through estimators that adapt to low-dimensional structure. Although these results are primarily included to complement our convergence theory of the DDIM sampler, they provide a rigorous statistical justification for the attainability of our score estimation assumptions.

Setting the stage, suppose we observe a dataset
X^{\mathsf{sample}}
containing
n
i.i.d. samples, namely,

X^{\mathsf{sample}}\coloneqq\big\{X_{i}^{\mathsf{sample}}\big\}_{i=1}^{n}\qquad\text{with }X_{i}^{\mathsf{sample}}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}p_{\mathsf{data}}.

(22)

The goal is to use these
samples to construct a collection of estimated score functions that approximate the true score functions

\{s_{t}^{\star}(\cdot)\}_{t=1}^{T}
.
We now show that score estimates satisfying Assumption
3
can indeed be achieved with finite-sample and adaptive guarantees.

Theorem 3

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Let

k^{\star}\coloneqq C_{\mathsf{cover}}k
, with the constant
C_{\mathsf{cover}}
defined in Assumption
2
. Let

\alpha_{t}=1-\beta_{t}
be chosen according to the schedule
(
9
), and recall the constant
c_{0}>0
in (
9
). Then there exists a score estimator

s_{t}(\cdot)
such that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddim}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{R_{0}^{k^{\star}+\frac{4}{k^{\star}+2}}\log^{2}(dR_{0}nT^{c_{0}})}{n}T^{c_{0}\left(\frac{k^{\star}}{2}+\frac{2}{k^{\star}+2}\right)},

(23a)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},1}^{2},\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Jacobi},2}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{R_{0}^{k^{\star}+\frac{16}{k^{\star}+4}}\log^{2}(dR_{0}nT^{c_{0}})}{n}T^{c_{0}\left(\frac{k^{\star}}{2}+\frac{8}{k^{\star}+4}\right)},

(23b)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Hess}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{R_{0}^{k^{\star}+\frac{36}{k^{\star}+6}}\log^{2}(dR_{0}nT^{c_{0}})}{n}T^{c_{0}\left(\frac{k^{\star}}{2}+1+\frac{18}{k^{\star}+6}\right)},

(23c)

\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddim}}v^{\top}\nabla s_{t}(x)v

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{4}\|v\|_{2}^{2}\qquad\text{for all }v,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

(23d)

At a high level, the score estimator
s_{t}
in the above theorem is the plug-in score induced by the
Gaussian kernel density estimator.
The detailed construction of
s_{t}
and the proof of
Theorem
3
are postponed to
Appendix
I.2
.

Regarding finite-sample guarantees for score estimation tailored to DDIM,
Cai and Li (2025)
established the first sample complexity result for the full-dimensional setting, showing a rate that scales as
T^{\Theta(d)}
and is nearly minimax optimal. To the best of our knowledge, however, no prior work has established a sample complexity guarantee for DDIM score estimation that accounts for the low-dimensional structure of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
.
When
p_{\mathsf{data}}
has intrinsic dimension
k
, it is natural to expect a minimax lower bound of order
T^{\Theta(k)}

(
Tang and Yang, 2022
;
Berenfeld and Hoffmann, 2019
, e.g.,)
. Our result matches this dependence in the exponent in an orderwise sence, thereby providing the first finite-sample guarantee that provably reveals low-dimensional adaptation in the score learning stage of DDIM.

Compared with prior score learning theory for DDPM, the main technical challenge is that our DDIM convergence analysis requires controlling higher-order score approximation errors, whose finite-sample analysis is substantially more involved than that of the standard
\ell_{2}
score estimation error. In addition, unlike
Cai and Li (2025)
, we need to establish the Hessian-based approximation guarantee required by our convergence analysis, which is essential for eliminating the dependence on the ambient dimension
d
.

3.2.2
DDPM theory under noisy scores

Turning attention to the DDPM-type samplers, this subsection establishes TV-based
convergence guarantees for DDPM in the absence of exact score functions. Compared with the DDIM case, we are in need of much simpler score estimation assumptions.

Assumption 4
(Score estimates for DDPM)

Let
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{score},t}^{2}\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}\big[\left\|{s_{t}(X_{t})-s_{t}^{\star}(X_{t})}\right\|_{2}^{2}\big]
, and
suppose that the estimated score functions
\{s_{t}(\cdot)\}_{t=1}^{T}
obey

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{{t}={1}}^{T}(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})\varepsilon_{\mathsf{score},t}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddpm}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}^{2}.

(24)

Note that
\ell_{2}
score estimation accuracy is commonly used in diffusion model theory (e.g.,
Chen et al. (2022a)
;
Benton et al. (2024)
).
This form of estimation error also aligns with practical training procedures such as score matching
(
Hyvärinen, 2005
;
Vincent, 2011
)
.

Convergence guarantees for DDPM.

We present below our TV-based convergence guarantees for the original DDPM sampler proposed by
Ho et al. (2020)
under noisy score estimates.
The proof can be found in Appendix
D
.

Theorem 4

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
, the DDPM sampler (
4
) with the coefficients
\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}=1-\alpha_{t}
and
\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}=\sqrt{\frac{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\alpha_{t})}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}
achieves

\displaystyle\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{1}},p_{Y_{1}})\lesssim\frac{k\log^{3}T}{T}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddpm}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}\sqrt{\log T}.

(25)

When score estimation is imperfect, our TV-based convergence guarantees degrade gracefully, with the bounds scaling linearly in
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddpm}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}
(a metric that measures the
\ell_{2}
estimation error).
In stark contrast to our DDIM theory in Theorem
2
, convergence of DDPM can be established under fewer assumptions; for instance, there is no need of imposing assumptions on the Jacobian or Hessian of score estimates as in Assumption
3
.
This favorable feature of DDPM arises since its stochastic update rule helps smooth the sampling trajectory, thereby eliminating the need to cope with many boundary cases.

As it turns out, the coefficients (
13
) are not the only choice of DDPM-type samplers that enable the desirable adaptation.
Our convergence theory can be extended to accommodate a broader set of coefficients, as stated below.
The proof is deferred to Appendix
D
.

Theorem 5

Suppose that the coefficients
\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}
and
\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}
satisfy

(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})\bigg(1-\frac{\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\bigg)^{2}=\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}-\big(\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}\big)^{2},\qquad t=1,\dots,T.

(26)

Also, assume that there exist some universal constants
C_{1},C_{2}\geq 1/2
such that

\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}\leq\min\left\{C_{1}(1-\alpha_{t}),C_{2}\sqrt{1-\alpha_{t}}\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}},\frac{1}{2}(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})\right\},\qquad t=1,\cdots,T.

(27)

Then under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
, the DDPM sampler (
4
) yields

\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{1}},p_{Y_{1}})\lesssim\frac{k\log^{3}T}{T}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddpm}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}\sqrt{\log T}.

Let us take a moment to discuss the range of coefficients satisfying relation (
26
).
Interestingly, this relation between
\eta_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}
and
\sigma_{t}^{\mathsf{ddpm}}
aligns perfectly with the set of coefficients discussed in
Song et al. (2020, Section 4.1)
.
More specifically,
Song et al. (2020, Eq. (12))
singled out the update rule below:

Y_{t-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}}\Big(Y_{t}-\sqrt{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\epsilon^{\mathsf{noise}}_{t}(Y_{t})+\sqrt{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}-\alpha_{t}\varsigma_{t}^{2}}\epsilon_{t}^{\mathsf{noise}}(Y_{t})+\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}\varsigma_{t}Z_{t}\Big)

(28)

for some coefficient
\varsigma_{t}
,
where
\epsilon_{t}^{\mathsf{noise}}(Y_{t})=-\sqrt{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}s_{t}(Y_{t})
serves as an estimate of the noise injected in the forward process.
One can demonstrate its equivalence with (
26
). The interested reader is referred to Appendix
E
for more details.

Remark 2

The differential-equation viewpoint explains why the DDIM and
generalized DDPM updates inherit low-dimensional adaptivity:
after rewriting the reverse ODE/SDE through Tweedie’s formula,
the essential drift term is governed by the posterior mean of

X_{0}
, which pulls the dynamics toward the
low-dimensional data support. We defer the full derivation
and the exact equivalence statements to
Appendix
A
.

Adaptive score estimation guarantee.

We now study the feasibility of constructing score estimators to satisfy Assumption
4
, again under the data generating model (
22
). Unlike DDIM, whose analysis requires higher-order conditions, the DDPM theory above only relies on
\ell_{2}
score matching errors. As a
result, we obtain a sharper finite-sample rate, as stated in the
following result.

Theorem 6

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Let

k^{\star}\coloneqq C_{\mathsf{cover}}k
, with the constant
C_{\mathsf{cover}}
defined in Assumption
2
. Let

\alpha_{t}=1-\beta_{t}
be chosen according to the schedule
(
9
), and recall the constant
c_{0}>0
in (
9
). Then there exists a score estimator

s_{t}(\cdot)
such that

\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddpm}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{R_{0}^{k^{\star}}\log(dR_{0}nT^{c_{0}})}{n}\min\left\{d^{\frac{2}{k^{\star}+2}}T^{c_{0}k^{\star}/2},R_{0}^{\frac{4}{k^{\star}+2}}T^{c_{0}\left(\frac{k^{\star}}{2}+\frac{2}{k^{\star}+2}\right)}\right\}.

In particular, since
0\leq 1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}\leq 1
, the same upper bound
also applies to
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{ddim}\text{-}\mathsf{sc}}^{2}
.

The score estimator in this theorem is a thresholded kernel-based score estimator.
Its detailed construction and the proof of
Theorem
6
are given in
Appendix
I.1
.

In contrast to the DDIM setting, where finite-sample approximation guarantees
that adapt to low-dimensional structure appear to be previously unavailable, several recent papers have studied the sample complexity of score learning for DDPM under
low-dimensional structure. Examples include

Azangulov et al. (2024)
and
Yakovlev and Puchkin (2025)
. The
former assumes that the data distribution is supported on a low-dimensional
manifold embedded in a high-dimensional ambient space, while the latter assumes
that samples are generated by first drawing from a low-dimensional cube and then
mapping the sample to the ambient space through a latent function. In contrast,
our result is built upon the more general intrinsic-dimension assumption (see Assumption
2
). Moreover, their approximation
guarantees contain terms that scale with

d^{O(k)}
, whereas the dependence on the ambient dimension
d
in our result is only

d^{2/(k^{\star}+2)}
. Consequently, when
k\gtrsim\log d
, this factor is of constant order, showing that, according to our theory, the score learning phase — and hence DDPM — fully adapts to the underlying low-dimensional structure in this regime.

3.3
Other alternatives of coefficient design?

Thus far, our main theorems (i.e., Theorems
2
-
5
) focus attention on specific coefficient choices as in the original DDIM and DDPM samplers. One might naturally wonder whether other coefficient choices could also facilitate low-dimensional adaptation capabilities.
As it turns out, these particular coefficients — or those exceedingly close to them — are almost necessary to achieve adaptivity, as explained in this subsection.

For simplicity, consider the case with accurate score estimates (i.e.,
s_{t}^{\star}=s_{t}
for all
t
), and let us look at the following mapping:

\Phi_{t}^{\star}(x,z)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{t}}}\big(x+\eta_{t}s_{t}^{\star}(x)+\sigma_{t}z\big).

(29)

Clearly, both the DDIM update rule (
10
) and DDPM update rule (
12
) in the
t
-th iteration can be described as
Y_{t-1}=\Phi_{t}^{\star}(Y_{t},Z_{t})
for some choices of
\eta_{t}
and
\sigma_{t}
(i.e.,
\sigma_{t}=0
for DDIM and
\sigma_{t}\neq 0
for DDPM), where
Z_{t}
is an independent standard Gaussian vector.
To evaluate how well the efficacy of DDIM-type and DDPM-type samplers, we propose to perform a sort of
one-step analysis
as follows:

1)

Start the sampler from
X_{t}
of the forward process (
1
);

2)

Compute one iteration
Y_{t-1}=\Phi_{t}^{\star}(Y_{t},Z_{t})
with an independent vector
Z_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
;

3)

Evaluate the TV distance between
Y_{t}
and
X_{t}
and see whether it is well-controlled.

An ideal sampler that can effectively adapt to unknown low dimensionality would not incur a TV distance blowing up with the ambient dimension
d
.

As it turns out, in order for the TV distance between
X_{t}
and
Y_{t}
to be well-controlled,
the coefficients
(\eta_{t},\sigma_{t})
must be carefully chosen, as revealed by the following lower bound.
The proof of this lower bound is provided in Appendix
G
.

Theorem 7

Consider any
k\leq d/2
, and
take the target distribution
p_{\mathsf{data}}
to be
{\mathcal{N}}\left(0,{\footnotesize\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{k}\\
&0\end{array}\right]}\right)
. Then for arbitrary choices of
(\eta_{t},\sigma_{t})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{TV}\big(\Phi_{t}^{\star}(X_{t},Z_{t}),X_{t-1}\big)\geq\frac{1}{100}\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{d}{2}}\left|\frac{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\left(1-\frac{\eta_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\right)^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}-1\right|,\,1\right\}.

(30)

In words, Theorem
7
asserts that even when initialized from a point from the true forward process, performing one iteration of DDIM/DDPM updates might already incur a TV distance that scales polynomially in the ambient dimension,
unless the coefficients are chosen to obey

\frac{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\left(1-\frac{\eta_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\right)^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}-1\approx 0.

(31)

•

Consider the DDIM-type sampler (
10
), which has
\sigma_{t}=0
. The requirement (
31
) then simplifies to

\eta_{t}\approx 1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}-\sqrt{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}=\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{1+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}},

the right-hand side of which is precisely the choice (
11a
) of the original DDIM sampler.

•

The DDPM-type sampler (
12
) then corresponds to the case with
\sigma_{t}>0
. Clearly, all coefficient choices studied in Theorem
5
satisfy this requirement, subsuming the choice (
13
) of the original DDPM sampler as a special case.

Somewhat surprisingly, while the original DDIM and DDPM update rules
(
Song et al., 2020
;
Ho et al., 2020
)
were derived heuristically (namely, by maximizing some variational lower bounds on the log-likelihoods) without any explicit consideration of the low-dimensional structure, the coefficients of the resulting algorithms prove to be nearly essential for adaptation to low dimensionality.

4
Related work

General convergence analysis of diffusion models.

Take the DDPM for instance, the work
Chen et al. (2022b)
established convergence analysis (based on Girsanov’s theorem) without assuming log-concavity; the smoothness assumption was further relaxed by
Lee et al. (2023)
;
Chen et al. (2023a)
.
A recent strand of work has been devoted to analyzing the convergence behavior of diffusion models
(
Chen et al., 2022b
;
Lee et al., 2022
;
Liu et al., 2022
;
Lee et al., 2023
;
Chen et al., 2023a
;
Benton et al., 2024
;
Chen et al., 2023d
;
Li et al., 2023a
;
Pedrotti et al., 2023
;
Cheng et al., 2023
;
Huang et al., 2024
;
Liang et al., 2024
;
Li and Yan, 2024b
;
Li et al., 2024d
;
Gao and Zhu, 2024
;
Gentiloni-Silveri and Ocello, 2025
)
; see
Tang and Zhao (2024)
for a tutorial.
Regarding the use of DDPM for a general class of non-smooth and non-log-concave distributions,
Benton et al. (2024)
established the best-known KL-based convergence guarantees, whereas the state-of-the-art TV-based convergence was derived by
Li and Yan (2024b)
. Turning attention to the DDIM,
Chen et al. (2023d)
derived the first polynomial-time analysis,
while
Chen et al. (2023c)
provided improved analysis for a variation of the probability flow ODE (by adding an additional stochastic step).

Li et al. (2023a)

improved the TV-based iteration complexity of the DDIM to
\widetilde{O}(d^{2}/\varepsilon)
, which was subsequently improved by
Li et al. (2024c)
to
\widetilde{O}(d/\varepsilon+d^{2})
.
Additionally, higher-order samplers tailored to solving the reverse-time SDE or probability flow ODE (e.g.,
Lu et al. (2022b)
;
Lu et al. (2022a)
) have been proven to achieve faster convergence

(
Li et al., 2024a
;
Wu et al., 2024b
;
Li and Cai, 2024
;
Huang et al., 2024
)
. Randomized midpoint methods have also been leveraged to provably speed up convergence
(
Gupta et al., 2024
;
Li and Jiao, 2024
)
. The convergence behavior of conditional diffusion models (or diffusion guidance) is another important topic that has been studied by several recent work (e.g.,
Wu et al. (2024a)
;
Chidambaram et al. (2024)
;
Chen et al. (2024a)
;
Fu et al. (2024)
).

Score matching.

An important stage of score-based generative modeling is score matching or score learning
(
Hyvärinen, 2005
;
Hyvärinen, 2007
;
Vincent, 2011
;
Song and Ermon, 2019
;
Ho et al., 2020
)
,
which aims to learn the score functions (typically using deep neural networks or transformers).
From the statistical perspectives,

Koehler et al. (2023)
characterized the asymptotic statistical efficiency of score matching,
while
Oko et al. (2023)
;
Wibisono et al. (2024)
;
Zhang et al. (2024)
;
Han et al. (2024)
;
Dou et al. (2024)
derived the statistical error rates and sample complexity for score matching.
Another recent work
Feng et al. (2024)
leveraged some idea from score matching to tackle convex M-estimation. We omit further details, as score matching is not our focus.

Diffusion models in the presence of low-dimensional structure.

Given the ubiquity of low-dimensional structure in practice
(
Pope et al., 2021
)
,
a recent line of research sought to unveil the role of low dimensionality in enabling more efficient data generation
(
Li and Yan, 2024a
;
Azangulov et al., 2024
;
Potaptchik et al., 2024
;
Huang et al., 2026
)
.
More concretely,
Li and Yan (2024a)
established the first iteration complexity upper bound for the DDPM that is adaptive to unknown low-dimensional structures, without the need of modifying the algorithm; the iteration complexity therein is proportional to
k^{4}
, with
k
the intrinsic dimension. This
k
-dependency was subsequently improved by
Azangulov et al. (2024)
to
k^{3}
and then tightened by
Potaptchik et al. (2024)
;
Huang et al. (2026)
to linear scaling. However, existing results establish only KL-based convergence, which can be overly conservative for TV guarantees via Pinsker’s inequality. Moreover, prior work does not address how ODE-based samplers adapt to unknown low-dimensional structure.

Apart from the above-mentioned convergence analysis for the sampling stage, the interplay between diffusion models and low-dimensional structure has been investigated from other perspectives as well
(
Chen et al., 2023b
;
Wang et al., 2024
;
Stanczuk et al., 2024
;
Mei and Wu, 2023
;
Li et al., 2023b
;
Azangulov et al., 2024
;
Li et al., 2024f
;
Li et al., 2024e
;
Cui et al., 2025
)
.
For instance,
Chen et al. (2023b)
considered the case where the target distribution lies on a linear subspace and developed sample complexity bounds for score matching that are independent of the ambient dimension.
Wang et al. (2024)
assumed the target distribution to be a mixture of low-rank Gaussians and explored the equivalence between score matching in this setting and subspace clustering.

Tang and Yang (2024)
studied the case when the data are supported on low-dimensional manifolds, and provided explicit convergence rates highlighting the importance of score estimation methods in such settings.
Stanczuk et al. (2024)
showed that diffusion models encode the data manifold by approximating its
normal bundle.
Moreover,
Li et al. (2023b)
established theoretical estimates of the generalization gap that evolves with the training dynamics of score-based diffusion models, suggesting a polynomially small generalization error that evades the curse of dimensionality.

5
Discussion

We have made progress towards understanding how diffusion models harness (unknown) low-dimensional structure to accelerate data generation.
For the DDIM sampler,
we have provided the first theoretical guarantee demonstrating its ability to adapt to low-dimensional structure.
When it comes to the DDPM sampler, we have improved the TV-based iteration complexity from
\widetilde{O}(k/\varepsilon^{2})

(
Potaptchik et al., 2024
;
Huang et al., 2026
)
to
\widetilde{O}(k/\varepsilon)
under exact scores. We have further extended these guarantees to the setting with noisy score estimates under suitable score estimation assumptions. Moreover, we have demonstrated that these assumptions can be achieved by score estimators with finite-sample guarantees that adapt to the underlying low-dimensional structure. Notably, our analysis covers the standard coefficient choices of
Ho et al. (2020)
;
Song et al. (2020)
, and our lower bounds highlight how these designs are essential for enabling low-dimensional adaptation.

Before concluding, we mention two directions for future work. First, the DDIM
coefficients in Theorem
2
are a special case of the
coefficient class in (
26
) used in
Theorem
5
. However, our DDPM analysis does not yet cover
the regime
\eta_{t}\gtrsim(1-\alpha_{t})\sigma_{t}^{2}
, leaving a gap between
the DDIM and DDPM theories. Closing this gap could lead to a more unified
framework for both samplers. Second, Theorem
7
gives a
single-step lower bound for the discretized reverse process. Extending it to
multi-step lower bounds for broader SDE/ODE-based samplers is another
interesting direction.

Appendix A
Interpretation from the lens of differential equations

In order to help elucidate why DDIM and DDPM are adaptive to low dimensionality,
we take a moment to derive their exact correspondence to reversed differential equations.
This viewpoint unearths the underlying forces that steer their trajectories toward the low-dimensional structure of interest, despite the effects of time discretization.
For convenience of presentation, we overload the notation by setting

\displaystyle\overline{\alpha}_{t}=\exp\left(-2\int_{0}^{t}\beta(s){\textnormal{d}}s\right),\qquad\text{for all }t\in[0,T],

(32)

where
\beta(t)
denotes the coefficient schedule in the forward SDE (
14
); as alluded to previously, this function
\overline{\alpha}_{t}
coincides with
\{\overline{\alpha}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
defined in (
8
) for the discrete-time process. In addition,
we recall from the Tweedie formula
(
Efron, 2011
)
that

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{\star}(x)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[X_{0}\,|\,X_{t}=x]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}\big(x+(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})s^{\star}_{t}(x)\big),

(33a)

and also introduce the noisy counterpart:

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}\big(x+(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})s_{t}(x)\big).

(33b)

In the sequel, we isolate several discretized differential equations that correpond exactly with the DDIM and DDPM samplers considered in the present paper.

\bullet

DDIM sampler.

The probability flow ODE (
17
) can be reparametrized by
\mu_{t}^{\star}
as follows using Tweedie’s formula (
33a
):

\displaystyle{\textnormal{d}}Y_{t}=\left(-\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}Y_{t}+\frac{\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}\mu^{*}_{T-t}(Y_{t})\right)\beta(T-t){\textnormal{d}}t,

where the drift term exhibits a semi-linear structure. To approximately solve this ODE, one can apply the exponential integrator scheme on the estimated semi-linear structure and select time discretization points as
t_{n}=n
for all
n=0,1,\cdots,T
, leading to the discretized dynamics below:

\displaystyle{\textnormal{d}}\widetilde{Y}_{t}=\left(-\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}\widetilde{Y}_{t}+\frac{\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}\mu_{T-t_{n}}(\widetilde{Y}_{t_{n}})\right)\beta(T-t){\textnormal{d}}t,\quad t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1}).

(34)

The DDIM sampler is intimately connected with this discretized dynamic, as asserted by the following proposition, whose proof can be found in Appendix
F.3
.

Proposition 1

The discretized process (
34
) is solved exactly by the DDIM update rule (
3
) with coefficient (
11a
) in the sense that
\widetilde{Y}_{n}=Y_{T-n}
for all
n=0,1,\dots,T
, provided that
\widetilde{Y}_{0}=Y_{T}
.

\bullet

(Generalized) DDPM sampler.

Similarly, the DDPM-type sampler — with the coefficients chosen as in Theorem
5
— can be exactly mapped to certain discretized differential equations.
More precisely, consider the generalized semi-linear SDE/ODE (
19
), which can be reparametrized via
\mu_{t}^{\star}
through Tweedie’s formula (
33a
):

\displaystyle{\textnormal{d}}Y_{t}=\biggl(-\frac{\xi(T-t)+\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}Y_{t}+\frac{\left(1+\xi(T-t)\right)\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}\mu^{\star}_{T-t}(Y_{t})\biggr)\beta(T-t){\textnormal{d}}t

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2\xi(Y-t)\beta(T-t)}\,{\textnormal{d}}W_{t}.

Adopting similar discretization scheme as in (
34
), we arrive at the following discretized process:

\displaystyle{\textnormal{d}}\widetilde{Y}_{t}=\biggl(-\frac{\xi(T-t_{n})+\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}\widetilde{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle+\frac{\left(1+\xi(T-t_{n})\right)\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{T-t}}\mu_{T-t_{n}}(\widetilde{Y}_{t_{n}})\biggr)\beta(T-t){\textnormal{d}}t

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2\xi(T-t_{n})\beta(T-t)}\,{\textnormal{d}}W_{t},\qquad t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1}),

(35)

where we recall that
t_{n}=n
.
Interestingly, the DDPM-type samplers considered in Theorem
5
correspond exactly to (
35
) with suitably chosen
\xi(t)
, as stated below. The proof of Proposition
2
can be found in Appendix
F.2
.

Proposition 2

The discretized process (
35
) can be solved exactly by the (generalized) DDPM update rule (
4
) — with coefficients satisfying (
26
) — in the sense that
\widetilde{Y}_{n}=Y_{T-n}
for all
n=0,1,\dots,T
, provided that
\widetilde{Y}_{0}=Y_{T}
and that the standard Gaussian vectors
\{Z_{t}\}
are chosen properly based on
(W_{t})
.

Remark 3

In the special case with coefficients (
13
),
the precise connection between such a discretized differential equation and the original DDPM sampler
(
Ho et al., 2020
)
has been discussed and utilized in the recent work
Azangulov et al. (2024)
;
Potaptchik et al. (2024)
;
Huang et al. (2026)
.

With the equivalent description (
34
) (resp. (
35
)) of the DDIM (resp. DDPM) sampler,
one can already gain insight into how these samplers adapt to unknown low-dimensional structure.
Suppose that we have access to accurate scores, so that
\mu^{\star}_{t}=\mu_{t}
. A closer inspection of (
34
) and (
35
) reveals that: the nonlinear components of the drift terms of both processes are proportional to
\mu^{\star}_{T-t_{n}}
,
which is defined as the conditional expectation of
X_{0}
(cf. (
33a
)). In other words, the most critical drift components take the form of conditional expectation of
X_{0}
, which inherently capture the low-dimensional structure of
p_{\mathsf{data}}
and steer the sampling dynamics towards this inherent structure.

Appendix B
Technical lemmas

In this section, we present several technical lemmas that prove useful for establishing our main theorems, with their proofs deferred to Appendix
H
.
For simplicity of presentation, we assume without loss of generality that
k\geq\log d
throughout the proof.

Before proceeding, let us introduce several notation that will be useful throughout.

•

For any covering scale
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that

\epsilon_{0}\leq c_{\epsilon}\,\frac{1\wedge\sqrt{k\log T/d}}{T}

for a sufficiently small universal constant
c_{\epsilon}>0
.
Let
\{x_{i}^{\star}\}_{1\leq i\leq N_{\epsilon_{0}}}
be an
\epsilon_{0}
-net of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{data}}
, with
N_{\epsilon_{0}}
denoting its cardinality.
Let
\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N_{\epsilon_{0}}}
be a disjoint
\epsilon_{0}
-cover for
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{data}}
such that
x_{i}^{\star}\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
for each
i
.
See, e.g.,
Vershynin (2018)
, for the definition of epsilon-net and epsilon-cover.

•

Define the following two sets:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\{1\leq i\leq N_{\epsilon_{0}}:\mathbb{P}(X_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{i})\geq\exp(-C_{1}k\log(R_{0}/\epsilon_{0}))\}

(36)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\biggl\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\omega\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{C_{1}k\log(R_{0}/\epsilon_{0})},

(37)

\displaystyle\left|{(x_{i}^{\star}-x_{j}^{\star})^{\top}\omega}\right|\leq\sqrt{C_{1}k\log(R_{0}/\epsilon_{0})}~\|x_{i}^{\star}-x_{j}^{\star}\|_{2},~\forall 1\leq i,j\leq N_{\epsilon_{0}}\biggr\}.

for some sufficiently large universal constant
C_{1}>0
. As it turns out,
\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{B}_{i}
and
\mathcal{G}
form certain high-probability sets related to the random vector
X_{0}\sim p_{\mathsf{data}}
and the standard Gaussian random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, respectively.

•

As mentioned previously in (
7
), we can express

X_{t}=\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}X_{0}+\sqrt{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}Z

(38)

for some random vector
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. By introducing

V_{\alpha}\coloneqq\sqrt{\alpha}V_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha}Z\qquad\text{with }V_{1}\coloneqq X_{0}

(39)

for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
,
we can write

X_{t}=V_{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}

(40)

for any
t
.
For every
\alpha\in[0,1-1/T]
, define a typical set for
V_{\alpha}
as follows:

\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\bigg\{\sqrt{\alpha}v_{1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,\omega:v_{1}\in\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{B}_{i},\omega\in\mathcal{G}\bigg\}.

(41)

•

Next, we turn to the posterior distribution of
V_{1}
given
V_{\alpha}
, which dictates the performance of DDIM and DDPM samplers. Let us introduce the following shorthand notation:

\displaystyle\mu_{V_{1}|V_{\alpha}}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[V_{1}\mid V_{\alpha}=v],

(42a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{V_{1}|V_{\alpha}}(v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\mathbb{E}}[V_{1}V_{1}^{\top}\mid V_{\alpha}=v]-\mu_{V_{1}|V_{\alpha}}(v)\mu_{V_{1}|V_{\alpha}}(v)^{\top}.

Given that the random objects
\mu_{V_{1}\mid V_{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}(V_{\overline{\alpha}_{t}})
and
\mathrm{Cov}_{V_{1}\mid V_{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}(V_{\overline{\alpha}_{t}})
will be used frequently throughout the proof, we shall often employ the following shorthand notation

\displaystyle\mu_{0|t}\coloneqq\mu_{V_{1}|V_{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{Cov}_{0|t}\coloneqq\mathrm{Cov}_{V_{1}|V_{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}

(42b)

as long as it is clear from the context.

•

In addition, we find it convenient to define

\varepsilon^{\mathsf{sc}}_{t}(x)\coloneqq s_{t}(x)-s_{t}^{\star}(x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varepsilon^{\mathsf{J}}_{t}(x)\coloneqq\frac{\partial s_{t}(x)}{\partial x}-\frac{\partial s_{t}^{\star}(x)}{\partial x}.

(43)

With the above set of notation in place, let us proceed to present a couple of technical lemmas.
While some of these proofs can be found in
Li and Yan (2024a)
;
Huang et al. (2026)
, we provide the proofs here for the sake of completeness.

The first lemma demonstrates that, for any
\alpha\in[0,1-1/T]
,
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
(cf. (
41
)) is a high-probability set for
V_{\alpha}
. The proof of this result is deferred to Appendix
H.1
.

Lemma 1

There exists some universal constant
C_{1}\geq 16C_{\mathsf{cover}}
such that for any
\alpha\in[0,1-1/T]

and any covering scale
\epsilon_{0}
,
the set
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
defined in (
41
) satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left(V_{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{C_{1}}{4}k\log(R_{0}/\epsilon_{0})\right).

Next, we develop a lemma that characterizes the concentration property of the point
V_{1}
given the observation
V_{\alpha}
; the proof can be found in Appendix
H.2
.

Lemma 2

For any given covering scale
\epsilon_{0}
,
consider any
v\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}
, and write
v=\sqrt{\alpha}v_{1}^{\star}+\sqrt{1-\alpha}\,\omega
for some
v_{1}^{\star}\in\bigcup_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
and
\omega\in{\mathcal{G}}
(cf. (
41
)). Suppose that
v_{1}^{\star}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{i(v)}
for some
i(v)\in\mathcal{I}
. Then there exists some universal constant
C_{2}>0
such that for any quantity
C\geq C_{2}
, one has

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\alpha}\,\big\|V_{1}-x^{\star}_{i(v)}\big\|\geq\sqrt{Ck(1-\alpha)\log(R_{0}/\epsilon_{0})}\mid V_{\alpha}=v\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{C}{20}k\log(R_{0}/\epsilon_{0})\right).

The above lemma has an immediate consequence that upper bounds on the moments of
V_{1}
under the posterior distribution
{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot\mid V_{\alpha}=v)
, provided that
v\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}
.
This is stated in the following corollary, whose proof (which is fairly straightforward) is omitted for the sake of brevity.

Corollary 1

There exists a universal constant
C_{3}>0
, such that for any
\alpha\in[0,1-1/T]
, the following inequalities hold for any point
v\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|{V_{1}-\mu_{V_{1}|V_{\alpha}}(v)}\right\|_{2}^{l}~\big|~V_{\alpha}=v\right]\leq C_{3}\left(\frac{1-{\alpha}}{{\alpha}}k\log T\right)^{l/2},\qquad l=1,2,3,4.

(44)

Moreover, we single out the lemma below that can help control the posterior covariance of interest. The proof is postponed to Appendix
H.3
.

Lemma 3

Suppose that Assumptions
2
and
1
hold. Denote
\widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}=\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t}(1-\alpha_{t})}{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}
. Then for any
t\geq 2
, the posterior covariance defined in (
42
) satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{t}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{X_{t}}\Big[\left\|{\mathrm{Cov}_{0|t}(X_{t})}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\Big]\leq 3\Big\{{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathrm{Cov}_{0|t}(X_{t})\big)\big]-{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathrm{Cov}_{0|t-1}(X_{t-1})\big)\big]\Big\}+\frac{3}{T^{10}}.

We also make note of the following basic property about
\{\alpha_{t}\}
(see
Li et al. (2024c, Section 5.1)
):

\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\leq\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t-1}}\leq\frac{4c_{1}\log T}{T}\qquad\text{for any }2\leq t\leq T.

(45)

The lemma below is a consequence of this property, whose proof can be found in Appendix
H.4
.

Lemma 4

There exists some universal constant
C_{6}>0
such that for any
t\geq 1
,

\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t}(1-\alpha_{t})}{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}-\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t+1}(1-\alpha_{t+1})}{(\alpha_{t+1}-\overline{\alpha}_{t+1})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t+1})}\leq\frac{C_{6}\log^{2}T}{T^{2}}\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}.

In addition, the lemma below helps one control the tightness of the Taylor expansion of a certain log-determinant function. Its proof is deferred to Appendix
H.5
.

Lemma 5

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be any positive semidefinite matrix, and let
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be any square matrix. Suppose that
\eta\|\Delta\|\leq 1/4
,
where
0<\eta<1
. Then it holds that

\log\det\left(I+\eta A+\eta\Delta\right)\geq\eta\big(\mathrm{tr}(A)+\mathrm{tr}(\Delta)\big)-4\eta^{2}\left(\left\|{A}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right).

Finally, we state below the Tweedie formula
(
Efron, 2011
)
, which establishes the intimate connection between the score function (resp. its corresponding Jacobian matrix) and the posterior mean (resp. posterior covariance) of
X_{0}
given
X_{t}
. For its proof, one can refer to
Robbins (1992)
.

\displaystyle s_{t}^{\star}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\mu_{0|t}(x_{t})-\frac{1}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}x_{t},

(46)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial s_{t}^{\star}(x_{t})}{\partial x_{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})^{2}}\mathrm{Cov}_{0|t}(x_{t})-\frac{1}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}I.

where

\displaystyle\mu_{0|t}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X_{0}\mid X_{t}=x_{t}],

(47a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{0|t}(x_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X_{0}X_{0}^{\top}\mid X_{t}=x_{t}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{0}\mid X_{t}=x_{t}]\,\mathbb{E}[X_{0}\mid X_{t}=x_{t}]^{\top}.

(47b)

Appendix C
Analysis for DDIM (proof of Theorem
2
)

In this section, we establish our convergence guarantees for the DDIM sampler as stated in Theorem
2
.

C.1
Main steps for proving Theorem
2

Our proof consists of several steps, which we present below.

Preparation.

Before proceeding to the proof, let us introduce a set of useful notation, to be used throughout this section. First, we define the following (deterministic) mapping:

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\alpha}_{t}}}\big(x+\eta_{t}s_{t}(x)\big)\qquad\text{with }\eta_{t}=\frac{1-\alpha_{t}}{1+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}}.

(48)

Lemma 6

For any
t=1,\cdots,T
, the mapping
{\Phi}_{t}
defined by (
48
) is a
C^{1}-
diffeomorphism on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

The proof of Lemma
6
is deferred to Appendix
C.2
.

Step 1: linking
\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{t-1}},p_{Y_{t-1}})
with
\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{t}},p_{Y_{t}})
.

To begin with, we single out
the following recursion that plays a pivotal role in our analysis: for any
t\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{t-1}},p_{Y_{t-1}})

\displaystyle{=}\sup\limits_{{\mathcal{A}}}\left\{{\mathbb{P}}_{X_{t-1}}({\mathcal{A}})-{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{t-1}}({\mathcal{A}})\right\}=\sup\limits_{{\mathcal{A}}}\left\{{\mathbb{P}}_{X_{t-1}}({\mathcal{A}})-{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{t}}\big(\Phi_{t}^{-1}({\mathcal{A}})\big)\right\}

\displaystyle{\leq}\sup\limits_{{\mathcal{A}}}\left\{{\mathbb{P}}_{X_{t-1}}({\mathcal{A}}){-}{\mathbb{P}}_{X_{t}}\big(\Phi_{t}^{-1}({\mathcal{A}})\big)\right\}{+}\sup\limits_{{\mathcal{A}}}\left\{{\mathbb{P}}_{X_{t}}\big(\Phi_{t}^{-1}({\mathcal{A}})\big){-}{\mathbb{P}}_{Y_{t}}\big(\Phi_{t}^{-1}({\mathcal{A}})\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{{\mathcal{A}}}\left\{{\mathbb{P}}_{X_{t-1}}({\mathcal{A}})-{\mathbb{P}}_{\Phi_{t}(X_{t})}({\mathcal{A}})\right\}+\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{t}},p_{Y_{t}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{t-1}},p_{\Phi_{t}(X_{t})})+\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{t}},p_{Y_{t}}),

(49)

where the first identity arises from the basic property of the TV distance
(
Tsybakov, 2009
)
.
This relation (
49
) underscores the importance of controlling

\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{t-1}},p_{\Phi_{t}(X_{t})})
when linking the TV distances of interest across two adjacent steps. Notably, in this extra TV distance term, the randomness of both
X_{t-1}
and
{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})
comes only from the forward process.

Step 2: identifying a crucial relation on the difference between
p_{X_{t-1}}
and
p_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}
.

In order to bound
\mathsf{TV}(p_{X_{t-1}},p_{\Phi_{t}(X_{t})})
, we need to examine the difference between
p_{X_{t-1}}
and
p_{\Phi_{t}(X_{t})}
. As it turns out, it would be helpful to first control the discrepancy between
p_{X_{t-1}}
and
p_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}
, given that
{\Phi}_{t}
exhibits some invertibility property when restricted to the set
\mathcal{E}_{t}
.

In view of Lemma
6

for any
x_{t-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, there exists a unique
x_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
obeying
{\Phi}_{t}(x_{t})=x_{t-1}
,
which in turn allows us to write

\displaystyle p_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}(x_{t-1})=p_{X_{t}}\big({\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x_{t-1})\big)\cdot\det\left(\frac{\partial{{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}}(x_{t-1})}{\partial x_{t-1}}\right)=p_{X_{t}}(x_{t})\cdot\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial x_{t-1}}\right).

(50)

Consequently, we can demonstrate that: for any
t\geq 2
and any
x_{t-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, it holds that

\displaystyle p_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}

\displaystyle(x_{t-1})-p_{X_{t-1}}(x_{t-1})=p_{X_{t}}(x_{t})\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial x_{t-1}}\right)-p_{X_{t-1}}(x_{t-1})

\displaystyle=\int\left\{p_{X_{t}\,|\,X_{0}}(x_{t}\,|\,x_{0})\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial x_{t-1}}\right)-p_{X_{t-1}\,|\,X_{0}}(x_{t-1}\,|\,x_{0})\right\}p_{X_{0}}(x_{0}){\textnormal{d}}x_{0}

\displaystyle=\int\Bigg\{1-\frac{p_{X_{t-1}\,|\,X_{0}}(x_{t-1}\,|\,x_{0})}{p_{X_{t}\,|\,X_{0}}(x_{t}\,|\,x_{0})}\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t-1}}{\partial x_{t}}\right)\Bigg\}p_{X_{t},X_{0}}(x_{t},x_{0})\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial x_{t-1}}\right){\textnormal{d}}x_{0}.

(51)

Moreover, recalling how
X_{t-1}
and
X_{t}
are generated, we can decompose

\displaystyle\frac{p_{X_{t-1}\,|\,X_{0}}(x_{t-1}\,|\,x_{0})}{p_{X_{t}\,|\,X_{0}}(x_{t}\,|\,x_{0})}\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t-1}}{\partial x_{t}}\right)=\frac{\left(\frac{1}{1{-}\overline{\alpha}_{t-1}}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\exp\Big\{\frac{{-}\|x_{t-1}{-}\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t-1}}x_{0}\|_{2}^{2}}{2(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t-1})}\Big\}}{\left(\frac{1}{1{-}\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\exp\Big\{\frac{{-}\|x_{t}{-}\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}x_{0}\|_{2}^{2}}{2(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}\Big\}}\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t-1}}{\partial x_{t}}\right)

(52)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\frac{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t-1}}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t-1}}{\partial x_{t}}\right)}_{\eqqcolon{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(x_{t},x_{0})}\underbrace{\exp\left\{\frac{\left\|{x_{t}-\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}x_{0}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}-\frac{\left\|{x_{t-1}-\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t-1}}x_{0}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t-1})}\right\}}_{\eqqcolon{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(x_{t},x_{0})}.

As a result, for any
x_{t-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle p_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}(x_{t-1})

\displaystyle-p_{X_{t-1}}(x_{t-1})

\displaystyle=\int\big(1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(x_{t},x_{0}){\mathcal{T}}_{2}(x_{t},x_{0})\big)p_{X_{t},X_{0}}(x_{t},x_{0})\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial x_{t-1}}\right){\textnormal{d}}x_{0}.

(53)

Step 3: calculating
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(x_{t},x_{0})
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(x_{t},x_{0})
.

In order to take advantage of the above relation (
53
),
an important task is to quantify the two terms
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(x_{t},x_{0})
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(x_{t},x_{0})
. For notational convenience, define

\displaystyle\xi_{t}(x_{t},x_{0})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(1{-}\frac{\eta_{t}}{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\right)\frac{\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\eta_{t}}{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}(x_{t}-\sqrt{\overline{\alpha}_{t}}x_{0})^{\top}\big(x_{0}-\mu_{0|t}(x_{t})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{-}\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t}\eta_{t}^{2}}{2(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})^{2}}\left\|{x_{0}-\mu_{0\,|\,t}(x_{t})}\right\|_{2}^{2}{+}\frac{\eta_{t}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}\big(\mu_{0|t}(x_{t})-x_{0}\big)^{\top}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{sc}}_{t}(x_{t})

\displaystyle\qquad{+}\left(1-\frac{\eta_{t}}{2(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}\right)\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t}\eta_{t}}{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}\mathrm{tr}\big(\mathrm{Cov}_{0\,|\,t}(x_{t})\big);

(54)

\displaystyle W_{t}(x_{t})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\log\det\left(I+\sqrt{\frac{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t}\eta_{t}}{(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})^{2}}\mathrm{Cov}_{0|t}(x_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}\eta_{t}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{J}}_{t}(x_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}}{2(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}\left\|{\varepsilon^{\mathsf{sc}}_{t}(x_{t})}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\sqrt{\frac{1-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}{\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t}}}\eta_{t}s_{t}^{\star}(x_{t})^{\top}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{sc}}_{t}(x_{t})

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{t}\eta_{t}}{(\alpha_{t}-\overline{\alpha}_{t})(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}\left(1-\frac{\eta_{t}}{2(1-\overline{\alpha}_{t})}\right)\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Cov}_{0|t}(x_{t})\right).

(55)

The following lemma, whose proof is deferred to Appendix
C.3
, establishes an intimate connection between
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2})
and
(\xi_{t},W_{t})
.

Lemma 7

The quantities
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(x_{t},x_{0})
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(x_{t},x_{0})
defined in (
52
) satisfy

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(x_{t},x_{0}){\mathcal{T}}_{2}(x_{t},x_{0})=e^{\xi_{t}(x_{t},x_{0})}\cdot e^{W_{t}(x_{t})}.

(56)

Further, it can be derived that

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}\xi_{t}(x_{t},x_{0})p_{X_{0}\,|\,X_{t}}(x_{0}\,|\,x_{t}){\textnormal{d}}x_{0}=0\qquad\text{for all }x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(57)

Step 4: bounding
p_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}-p_{X_{t-1}}
.

We can now invoke the preceding results to upper bound
p_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}-p_{X_{t-1}}
. Focusing on any
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, one can put
(
53
) and Lemma
7
together to show that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}({\mathcal{A}})-

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{X_{t-1}}({\mathcal{A}})=\int_{{\mathcal{A}}}\left\{p_{{\Phi}_{t}(X_{t})}(x_{t-1})-p_{X_{t-1}}(x_{t-1})\right\}{\textnormal{d}}x_{t-1}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(a)}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\{1-{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(x_{t},x_{0}){\mathcal{T}}_{2}(x_{t},x_{0})\big\}p_{X_{t},X_{0}}(x_{t},x_{0})\det\left(\frac{\partial x_{t}}{\partial x_{t-1}}\right){\textnormal{d}}x_{0}{\textnormal{d}}x_{t-1}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(b)}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}({\mathcal{A}})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{1-e^{\xi_{t}(x_{t},x_{0})}\cdot e^{W_{t}(x_{t})}\right\}p_{X_{t},X_{0}}(x_{t},x_{0}){\textnormal{d}}x_{0}{\textnormal{d}}x_{t}

\displaystyle=
